Dynamical analysis and soliton solutions to (3+1)-dimensional extended quantum nonlinear Zakharov–Kuznetsov equation
Authors: Mehreen Fatima, Muhammad Abbas, M. Higazy, Muhammad Nadeem Anwar, Muhammad Zain Yousaf, Asnake Birhanu, Y. S. Hamed
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
This study employs an improved modified extended tanh-function method to address the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3+1)$$\end{document}-dimensional extended quantum nonlinear Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation, which models nonlinear wave propagation in quantum plasmas and magnetized fluid systems. The proposed method uses a generalized quartic-root auxiliary equation to derive kink, anti-kink, bright/dark solitons, Jacobi elliptic, Weierstrass doubly periodic, and complex hybrid solutions within a unified framework. To visually analyze the characteristics of obtained solutions, 3D, 2D and contour plots are plotted. Beyond exact analytical solutions, a dynamical system is obtained by using Galilean transformation, along with detailed bifurcation diagrams. To analyze the chaotic behavior and sensitivity of the system on initial conditions, comparative time-series evolution and 2D, 3D phase-space trajectories are illustrated. This work not only adds to the collection of analytical tools developed for high-dimensional nonlinear evolution equations but also forms a bridge between symbolic solution construction and theory of nonlinear dynamical systems.

Introduction

Nonlinear evolution equations (NLEEs) are the foundation for describing the dynamic behavior of complex systems in physics, engineering, and biology1–3. Unlike linear models, NLEEs preserve the inherent non-proportionality between input and output, which typically yields rich and interesting phenomena like solitons, chaos, and pattern formation. These relations are crucial for describing wave propagation in a wide variety of applications, ranging from fluid dynamics and quantum mechanics to plasma physics and optical communication4. Nonlinear wave phenomena in quantum plasmas is one of the most important areas of research in contemporary physics. It gives us insight into intricate phenomena in extreme conditions like white dwarfs, neutron stars, and strong laser-plasma interactions, where quantum effects cannot be neglected. In such systems, particles are packed so tightly that classical physics cannot account for their behavior5,6. One of the most important NLEEs is the Zakharov-Kuznetsov (ZK) equation, first proposed in 1974 by Zakharov and Kuznetsov7. It accounts for the weakly nonlinear ion-acoustic wave propagation in a magnetized plasma of cold ions and hot isothermal electrons. Being an extension of the Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) equation to more dimensions, the ZK equation also includes transverse perturbations and, therefore, better fits the description of wave dynamics in real physical environments like space plasmas and some fluid systems8,9. When quantum effects are considered, this equation is the quantum Zakharov–Kuznetsov (QZK) equation. It models how nonlinear ion-acoustic waves travel in a quantum plasma consisting of cold ions and hot electrons under a magnetic field10,11. Moslem et al.12 first investigated the QZK equation, giving it basic insights into solitary and periodic solutions. Higher-order nonlinear and dispersive terms were also included in further generalizations. It resulted in the derivation of the nonlinear extended quantum Zakharov-Kuznetsov (NLEQZK) equation, initially established by Sabry et al.13 by employing the reductive perturbation method to the equations of quantum hydrodynamics and Poisson. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3+1)$$\end{document}-dimensional version of this equation is crucial in describing dynamics of quantum ion-acoustic waves in magnetized quantum plasmas, such as electron-positron-ion plasmas and quantum fluids like Bose-Einstein condensates14.The general form of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3+1)$$\end{document}-dimensional NLEQZK equation15 can be expressed as:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U_t + \left( a \, U + b \, U^2\right) \, U_z + c \, U_{zzz} + d \left( U_{xx} + U_{yy}\right) _z = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=U(x,y,z,t)$$\end{document} is the electrostatic potential, and a, b, c, d are arbitrary constants that are associated with physical parameters like the quantum diffraction effect, ion cyclotron frequency, and electron Fermi energy. Their signs and magnitudes may differ depending on the physical regime under consideration. Thus, no restrictive constraints are imposed to preserve the model’s generality. Recent studies have further extended the analysis of multidimensional ZK-type models using both analytical and computational approaches. Towhiduzzaman et al.16 used physics-informed neural networks to study the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3+1)$$\end{document}-dimensional Zakharov-Kuznetsov-Burgers equation and reported rich lump and soliton dynamics in plasma environments. Kaur and Wazwaz17,18, investigated lump, breather, and solitary wave structures in related higher-dimensional nonlinear systems, while Bruzón et al.19 focused on travelling-wave solutions of modified ZK equations.Together, these contributions underline the ongoing interest in multidimensional nonlinear wave patterns and provide further motivation for the detailed study of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3+1)$$\end{document}-dimensional NLEQZK model carried out in the present work. Various analytical approaches have been used to find exact solutions of the NLEQZK equation over the years. These include the generalized Kudryashov method and modified Khater method, Sardar sub-equation method20, extended simplest equation method21, and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{G'}{G})$$\end{document}-expansion approach22. These methods have produced a broad variety of solutions including dark, bright, singular, periodic, kink, and anti-kink solitons, each providing information on the intricate behavior of nonlinear waves in quantum plasmas. In spite of these developments, there is a demand for more systematic and potent techniques that can produce a broad variety of exact solutions with high efficiency.

The improved modified extended tanh-function method (IMETFM), as a strong extension of the extended and modified tanh-function methodologies23,24, provides a generalized framework to create various traveling wave solutions.The modified extended tanh-function method24 uses a lower-order auxiliary equation and is essentially restricted to hyperbolic and trigonometric wave profiles. In contrast, more recent work has confirmed the effectiveness of IMETFM25 for high-dimensional nonlinear systems. This provides a strong rationale for applying IMETFM to the NLEQZK equation, a sophisticated quantum plasma model that demands a powerful analytical approach. Based on a Ricatti-type auxiliary equation, this approach facilitates the derivation of hyperbolic, trigonometric, rational, and Weierstrass elliptic functions solutions through the adjustment of its parameters. Its algorithmic framework enables it to combine some of the current techniques-like the conventional tanh approach and the (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{G'}{G}$$\end{document}) -expansion approach into one versatile procedure. With its great solution capability and flexibility, it has been effectively utilized in a broad range of nonlinear models, such as higher-order nonlinear equations and quantum plasma wave equations, with the ability to generate a richer set of solutions than less complex methods.Encouraged by its performance and generic structure, for the first time in the current paper, we use the IMETFM to solve NLEQZK model. To our knowledge, the proposed technique has not yet been utilized to obtain exact solutions of the given model, and this is a new addition to the research branch of quantum plasma wave dynamics. In addition to constructing exact traveling-wave solutions, we reduce the governing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3 + 1)$$\end{document}-dimensional NLEQZK equation to an associated traveling wave ordinary differential equation and then perform the dynamical analysis on this reduced system. In particular, all bifurcation, chaotic behavior, and sensitivity results reported in this work pertain to this one-dimensional traveling wave dynamical system rather than the full partial differential equation. Furthermore, bifurcation refers to qualitative changes in the behavior of a system as a parameter is varied, often leading to multiple stable states or patterns. Chaotic behavior is characterized by apparent randomness and complex dynamics that arise from deterministic nonlinear systems. Sensitivity, especially to initial conditions, means small changes can lead to vastly different outcomes, making prediction challenging. Our goal is to derive a complete set of novel exact traveling wave solutions, such as solitons, periodic waves, rational solutions, and elliptic-type structures. Further, bifurcation analysis will be conducted on the considered equation to understand its dynamic behaviors in detail.Paper is organized in different sections. In “Summary of method”, the method is defined. “Mathematical modeling” deals with mathematical modelling. In “Implementation of method”, method is implemented to the considered equation. Visual evaluation and physical description of obtained solutions are discussed in “Visual evaluation and discussion” and “Physical interpretation of obtained solutions and application”, respectively. Furthermore, a comparison between applied methods is analyzed in “Comparison”. “Bifurcation”, “Chaotic behaviour” and “Sensitivity analysis” are devoted to bifurcation, chaotic behaviour, and sensitivity analysis. Finally, “Conclusion” briefly concludes the study.

Summary of method

The steps by step procedure of IMETFM25 is described in this section. Step 1.Consider NLPDE of the form: 2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(U, U_{t}, U_{x}, U_{y}, U_{z}, U_{xt}, U_{xx}, U_{xy}, U_{xz}, U_{yy}, U_{yz}, U_{xxx}, \dots ) = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where, P is the polynomial expression in unknown function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=U(x,y,t,z)$$\end{document} along with its partial derivatives. Now, transformation 3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U(x,y,z,t) = u(\xi ), \quad \xi = \delta x + \beta y + \mu z - \omega t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} is applied to reduce NLPDE to ordinary differential equation (ODE) of the form: 4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(u, u', u'', u'', u''', \dots ) = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Step 2ODE has exact solution of the form: 5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(\xi ) = \sum _{i=0}^{N} A_i G(\xi )^i + \sum _{j=1}^{N} B_j G(\xi )^{-j}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_i (0 \le i \le N)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_j (1 \le j \le N)$$\end{document} are constants. And \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(\xi )$$\end{document} satisfied following auxiliary equation: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G(\xi )' = \alpha \sqrt{ \gamma _0 + \gamma _1 G + \gamma _2 G^2 + \gamma _3 G^3 + \gamma _4 G^4}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = \pm 1$$\end{document}. The auxiliary parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0, \gamma _1, \gamma _2, \gamma _3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _4$$\end{document} are dimensionless integration constants.Step 3The value of N is obtained by balancing highest-order derivative with highest nonlinear term. i.e homogeneous balancing principle.Step 4Solution of Eq. (4) is obtained by putting value of N in Eq. (5). Now put obtained solution along with auxiliary equation in Eq. (4). Then system of equation is obtained by setting all coefficient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(\xi )^i$$\end{document} equal zero. Then values of constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_i (0 \le i \le N)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_j (1 \le j \le N)$$\end{document} are obtained by solving algebraic system.Step 5Depending on the values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_i$$\end{document}’s and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_j$$\end{document}’s, the Eq. (4) has following solutions: Under condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = \gamma _3 = 0$$\end{document}, the following solutions are obtained: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_1(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{\gamma _4}} \, \textrm{sech}\left( \xi \sqrt{\gamma _2} \right) , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4< 0, \\ G_2(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{\gamma _4}} \, \textrm{sec}\left( \xi \sqrt{-\gamma _2} \right) , \quad \gamma _2 < 0,\; \gamma _4> 0, \\ G_3(\xi )&= \frac{-\alpha }{\xi \sqrt{\gamma _4}}, \quad \gamma _4 > 0,\; \gamma _2 = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1 = g_3 = 0$$\end{document}, then solutions are: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_3(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{2\gamma _4}} \, \tanh \left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2}{4\gamma _4},\; \gamma _2< 0,\; \gamma _4> 0, \\ G_4(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2\gamma _4}} \, \tan \left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4> 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2}{4\gamma _4}, \\ G_5(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (2m^2 - 1)}} \, \textrm{cn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2m^2 - 1}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4< 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 m^2 (1 - m^2)}{\gamma _4 (2m^2 - 1)^2}, \\ G_6(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{m^2}{\gamma _4 (2 - m^2)}} \, \textrm{dn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2 - m^2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4< 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 (1 - m^7)}{\gamma _4 (2 - m^2)^2}, \\ G_8(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (m^2 + 1)}} \, \textrm{sn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{m^2 + 1}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2 < 0,\; \gamma _4 > 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (m^2 + 1)^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _2 = \gamma _4 = 0$$\end{document}, then obtained solution is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_9(\xi ) = \wp \left( \frac{\xi \sqrt{\gamma _3}}{2}, -\frac{4\gamma _1}{\gamma _3}, -\frac{4\gamma _0}{\gamma _3} \right) , \quad \gamma _3 > 0,\; \gamma _0 \ne 0,\; \gamma _1 \ne 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\wp$$\end{document} is the Weierstrass elliptic function.The following solutions are obtained by considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = \gamma _2 = 0$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{10}(\xi )&= \frac{4\gamma _3}{\gamma _3 \xi ^2 - 4\gamma _4}, \quad \gamma _4 \ne 0, \\ G_{11}(\xi )&= \frac{\gamma _3}{2\gamma _4} e^{\frac{\alpha \sqrt{\gamma _3}}{2\sqrt{-\gamma _4}} \xi }, \quad \gamma _4 < 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _3 = \gamma _4 = 0$$\end{document}, then solutions become: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{12}(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _0}{\gamma _2}} \, \sin \left[ \xi \sqrt{-\gamma _2} \right] , \quad \gamma _1 = 0,\; \gamma _0> 0,\; \gamma _2< 0, \\ G_{13}(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _0}{\gamma _2}} \, \sinh \left[ \xi \sqrt{-\gamma _2} \right] , \quad \gamma _1 = 0,\; \gamma _0> 0,\;\gamma 2 < 0, \\ G_{14}(\xi )&= e^{\alpha \xi \sqrt{\gamma _2}} - \frac{\gamma _1}{2\gamma _2}, \quad \gamma _2 > 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _1^2}{4\gamma _2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The solution obtained by considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _4 > 0$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{15}(\xi ) = \frac{\alpha }{2} \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{\gamma _4}} \left[ \tanh \left( \frac{\xi \sqrt{\gamma _2}}{2} \right) + 1 \right] , \quad \gamma _2 > 0,\; \gamma _3 = 2\alpha \sqrt{\gamma _2 \gamma _4}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Step 1.Consider NLPDE of the form: 2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(U, U_{t}, U_{x}, U_{y}, U_{z}, U_{xt}, U_{xx}, U_{xy}, U_{xz}, U_{yy}, U_{yz}, U_{xxx}, \dots ) = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where, P is the polynomial expression in unknown function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=U(x,y,t,z)$$\end{document} along with its partial derivatives. Now, transformation 3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U(x,y,z,t) = u(\xi ), \quad \xi = \delta x + \beta y + \mu z - \omega t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} is applied to reduce NLPDE to ordinary differential equation (ODE) of the form: 4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(u, u', u'', u'', u''', \dots ) = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Consider NLPDE of the form: 2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(U, U_{t}, U_{x}, U_{y}, U_{z}, U_{xt}, U_{xx}, U_{xy}, U_{xz}, U_{yy}, U_{yz}, U_{xxx}, \dots ) = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where, P is the polynomial expression in unknown function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=U(x,y,t,z)$$\end{document} along with its partial derivatives. Now, transformation 3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U(x,y,z,t) = u(\xi ), \quad \xi = \delta x + \beta y + \mu z - \omega t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} is applied to reduce NLPDE to ordinary differential equation (ODE) of the form: 4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(u, u', u'', u'', u''', \dots ) = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Step 2ODE has exact solution of the form: 5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(\xi ) = \sum _{i=0}^{N} A_i G(\xi )^i + \sum _{j=1}^{N} B_j G(\xi )^{-j}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_i (0 \le i \le N)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_j (1 \le j \le N)$$\end{document} are constants. And \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(\xi )$$\end{document} satisfied following auxiliary equation: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G(\xi )' = \alpha \sqrt{ \gamma _0 + \gamma _1 G + \gamma _2 G^2 + \gamma _3 G^3 + \gamma _4 G^4}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = \pm 1$$\end{document}. The auxiliary parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0, \gamma _1, \gamma _2, \gamma _3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _4$$\end{document} are dimensionless integration constants.

Under condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = \gamma _3 = 0$$\end{document}, the following solutions are obtained: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_1(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{\gamma _4}} \, \textrm{sech}\left( \xi \sqrt{\gamma _2} \right) , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4< 0, \\ G_2(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{\gamma _4}} \, \textrm{sec}\left( \xi \sqrt{-\gamma _2} \right) , \quad \gamma _2 < 0,\; \gamma _4> 0, \\ G_3(\xi )&= \frac{-\alpha }{\xi \sqrt{\gamma _4}}, \quad \gamma _4 > 0,\; \gamma _2 = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1 = g_3 = 0$$\end{document}, then solutions are: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_3(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{2\gamma _4}} \, \tanh \left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2}{4\gamma _4},\; \gamma _2< 0,\; \gamma _4> 0, \\ G_4(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2\gamma _4}} \, \tan \left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4> 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2}{4\gamma _4}, \\ G_5(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (2m^2 - 1)}} \, \textrm{cn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2m^2 - 1}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4< 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 m^2 (1 - m^2)}{\gamma _4 (2m^2 - 1)^2}, \\ G_6(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{m^2}{\gamma _4 (2 - m^2)}} \, \textrm{dn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2 - m^2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4< 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 (1 - m^7)}{\gamma _4 (2 - m^2)^2}, \\ G_8(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (m^2 + 1)}} \, \textrm{sn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{m^2 + 1}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2 < 0,\; \gamma _4 > 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (m^2 + 1)^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1 = g_3 = 0$$\end{document}, then solutions are: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_3(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{2\gamma _4}} \, \tanh \left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2}{4\gamma _4},\; \gamma _2< 0,\; \gamma _4> 0, \\ G_4(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2\gamma _4}} \, \tan \left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4> 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2}{4\gamma _4}, \\ G_5(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (2m^2 - 1)}} \, \textrm{cn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2m^2 - 1}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4< 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 m^2 (1 - m^2)}{\gamma _4 (2m^2 - 1)^2}, \\ G_6(\xi )&= \sqrt{-\frac{m^2}{\gamma _4 (2 - m^2)}} \, \textrm{dn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{2 - m^2}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2> 0,\; \gamma _4< 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 (1 - m^7)}{\gamma _4 (2 - m^2)^2}, \\ G_8(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (m^2 + 1)}} \, \textrm{sn}\left[ \xi \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _2}{m^2 + 1}} \right] , \quad \gamma _2 < 0,\; \gamma _4 > 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _2^2 m^2}{\gamma _4 (m^2 + 1)^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _2 = \gamma _4 = 0$$\end{document}, then obtained solution is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_9(\xi ) = \wp \left( \frac{\xi \sqrt{\gamma _3}}{2}, -\frac{4\gamma _1}{\gamma _3}, -\frac{4\gamma _0}{\gamma _3} \right) , \quad \gamma _3 > 0,\; \gamma _0 \ne 0,\; \gamma _1 \ne 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\wp$$\end{document} is the Weierstrass elliptic function.

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _2 = \gamma _4 = 0$$\end{document}, then obtained solution is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_9(\xi ) = \wp \left( \frac{\xi \sqrt{\gamma _3}}{2}, -\frac{4\gamma _1}{\gamma _3}, -\frac{4\gamma _0}{\gamma _3} \right) , \quad \gamma _3 > 0,\; \gamma _0 \ne 0,\; \gamma _1 \ne 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\wp$$\end{document} is the Weierstrass elliptic function.

The following solutions are obtained by considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = \gamma _2 = 0$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{10}(\xi )&= \frac{4\gamma _3}{\gamma _3 \xi ^2 - 4\gamma _4}, \quad \gamma _4 \ne 0, \\ G_{11}(\xi )&= \frac{\gamma _3}{2\gamma _4} e^{\frac{\alpha \sqrt{\gamma _3}}{2\sqrt{-\gamma _4}} \xi }, \quad \gamma _4 < 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

The following solutions are obtained by considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = \gamma _2 = 0$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{10}(\xi )&= \frac{4\gamma _3}{\gamma _3 \xi ^2 - 4\gamma _4}, \quad \gamma _4 \ne 0, \\ G_{11}(\xi )&= \frac{\gamma _3}{2\gamma _4} e^{\frac{\alpha \sqrt{\gamma _3}}{2\sqrt{-\gamma _4}} \xi }, \quad \gamma _4 < 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _3 = \gamma _4 = 0$$\end{document}, then solutions become: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{12}(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _0}{\gamma _2}} \, \sin \left[ \xi \sqrt{-\gamma _2} \right] , \quad \gamma _1 = 0,\; \gamma _0> 0,\; \gamma _2< 0, \\ G_{13}(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _0}{\gamma _2}} \, \sinh \left[ \xi \sqrt{-\gamma _2} \right] , \quad \gamma _1 = 0,\; \gamma _0> 0,\;\gamma 2 < 0, \\ G_{14}(\xi )&= e^{\alpha \xi \sqrt{\gamma _2}} - \frac{\gamma _1}{2\gamma _2}, \quad \gamma _2 > 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _1^2}{4\gamma _2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _3 = \gamma _4 = 0$$\end{document}, then solutions become: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{12}(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{-\frac{\gamma _0}{\gamma _2}} \, \sin \left[ \xi \sqrt{-\gamma _2} \right] , \quad \gamma _1 = 0,\; \gamma _0> 0,\; \gamma _2< 0, \\ G_{13}(\xi )&= \pm \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _0}{\gamma _2}} \, \sinh \left[ \xi \sqrt{-\gamma _2} \right] , \quad \gamma _1 = 0,\; \gamma _0> 0,\;\gamma 2 < 0, \\ G_{14}(\xi )&= e^{\alpha \xi \sqrt{\gamma _2}} - \frac{\gamma _1}{2\gamma _2}, \quad \gamma _2 > 0,\; \gamma _0 = \frac{\gamma _1^2}{4\gamma _2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

The solution obtained by considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _4 > 0$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{15}(\xi ) = \frac{\alpha }{2} \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{\gamma _4}} \left[ \tanh \left( \frac{\xi \sqrt{\gamma _2}}{2} \right) + 1 \right] , \quad \gamma _2 > 0,\; \gamma _3 = 2\alpha \sqrt{\gamma _2 \gamma _4}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

The solution obtained by considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _4 > 0$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{15}(\xi ) = \frac{\alpha }{2} \sqrt{\frac{\gamma _2}{\gamma _4}} \left[ \tanh \left( \frac{\xi \sqrt{\gamma _2}}{2} \right) + 1 \right] , \quad \gamma _2 > 0,\; \gamma _3 = 2\alpha \sqrt{\gamma _2 \gamma _4}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Mathematical modeling

The NLEQZK equation Eq. (1) is a three-dimensional NLPDE that models the evolution of ion-acoustic waves in magnetized quantum plasma. The quantum corrections and nonlinear dispersive effects are incorporated in the model, taking the classical ZK framework to a more complex level of plasma dynamics in three spatial dimensions and time. The term by term physical interpretation of equation is given in Table 1. Apply travelling transformation

Physical interpretation of terms in the NLEQZK equation.

Implementation of method

NLEQZK equation is analyzed in this section by using IMETFM. In considered equation, the nonlinear term behave as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u^{3}(\xi ) \sim G^{3N}(\xi ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}On the other hand, using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(\xi ) \sim G^{N}(\xi )$$\end{document}, and differentiating twice, the highest derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u''(\xi )$$\end{document} comes from differentiating the leading power of G, which gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u''(\xi ) \sim \frac{d^2}{d\xi ^2}G^{N}(\xi ) \sim G^{N+2}(\xi ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Now, balancing the dominant powers yields relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3N=N+2$$\end{document}, which gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=1$$\end{document}. Now Eq. (6) has solution of the form,7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(\xi ) = A_0 + A_1 G(\xi ) + \frac{B_1 }{G(\xi )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Now, put this solution along with auxiliary equation in Eq. (6) and collecting all the coefficient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(\xi )^i (i=0,1,2,3,4,5,6)$$\end{document} and setting them equal to zero, following algebraic system is obtained:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (G(\xi ))^0:-2b\mu B_1^3 - 12d\alpha ^2\beta ^2\mu B_1 \gamma _0 - 12d\alpha ^2\delta ^2\mu B_1 \gamma _0 - 12c\alpha ^2\mu ^3 B_1 \gamma _0,\\ (G(\xi ))^1:-3a\mu B_1^2 - 6b\mu A_0 B_1^2 - 9d\alpha ^2\beta ^2\mu B_1\gamma _1 - 9d\alpha ^2\delta ^2\mu B_1\gamma _1 - 9c\alpha ^2\mu ^3 B_1\gamma _1,\\ (G(\xi ))^2:6\omega B_1 - 6a\mu A_0 B_1 - 6b\mu A_0^2 B_1 - 6b\mu A_1 B_1^2 - 6d\alpha ^2\beta ^2\mu B_1\gamma _2 - 6d\alpha ^2\delta ^2\mu B_1\gamma _2 - 6c\alpha ^2\mu ^3 B_1\gamma _2,\\ (G(\xi ))^3:6\omega A_0 - 3a\mu A_0^2 - 2b\mu A_0^3 - 6a\mu A_1 B_1 - 12b\mu A_0 A_1 B_1 - 3d\alpha ^2\beta ^2\mu A_1\gamma _1 - 3d\alpha ^2\delta ^2\mu A_1\gamma _1 - 3c\alpha ^2\mu ^3 A_1\gamma _1\\ \hspace{2cm} - 3d\alpha ^2\beta ^2\mu B_1\gamma _3 - 3d\alpha ^2\delta ^2\mu B_1\gamma _3 - 3c\alpha ^2\mu ^3 B_1\gamma _3, \\ (G(\xi ))^4:6\omega A_1 - 6a\mu A_0 A_1 - 6b\mu A_0^2 A_1 - 6b\mu A_1^2 B_1 - 6d\alpha ^2\beta ^2\mu A_1\gamma _2 - 6d\alpha ^2\delta ^2\mu A_1\gamma _2 - 6c\alpha ^2\mu ^3 A_1\gamma _2,\\ (G(\xi ))^5: -3a\mu A_1^2 - 6b\mu A_0 A_1^2 - 9d\alpha ^2\beta ^2\mu A_1\gamma _3 - 9d\alpha ^2\delta ^2\mu A_1\gamma _3 - 9c\alpha ^2\mu ^3 A_1\gamma _3,\\ (G(\xi ))^6: -2b\mu A_1^3 - 12d\alpha ^2\beta ^2\mu A_1\gamma _4 - 12d\alpha ^2\delta ^2\mu A_1\gamma _4 - 12c\alpha ^2\mu ^3 A_1\gamma _4.\\ \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Now, solutions are obtained by using cases. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0 = \gamma _1 = \gamma _3 = 0$$\end{document}, following set of coefficients is obtained by solving system of equations given in Eq. Visual evaluation and discussion

The IMETFM effectively produced a rich variety of exact traveling wave solutions to NLEQZK equation. Their graphical presentations, shown via 3D surface, 2D and contour plots for a given set of parameters, exhibit different nonlinear wave profiles. These are the kink, anti-kink, W-shaped, periodic, bell-shaped, and anti-bell-shaped solitons. The visualizations graphically display the typical form of each wave type, ranging from the topological transition of kinks to the localized pulse of a bell soliton and the oscillatory behavior of the periodic wave. The presence of such a diverse range of solutions emphasizes the richness and complexity inherent in the quantum plasma system depicted by this higher-dimensional model (Figs. 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9).Fig. 1Bright soliton solution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1(\xi )$$\end{document} represent 3D, 2D and contour plots by using parameters: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \begin{document}$$\delta = -0.2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _2 = 0.75$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _4 = -2$$\end{document}, (17).

Physical interpretation of obtained solutions and application

The IMETFM has been implemented successfully to NLEQZK equation, which provides an extensive range of exact traveling wave solutions. These cover significant nonlinear waveforms such as kink, anti-kink, W-shaped, periodic, the bell-shaped (bright), as well as the anti-bell-shaped (dark) solitons. Graphical illustrations through 2D plots, 3D surfaces, and contour plots for chosen parameter values not only verify the mathematical correctness of the solutions but also provide further physical insight into electrostatic wave behavior in dense, magnetized quantum plasmas. Beyond theory, these wave structures are relevant in the real world, with applications in astronomy (neutron star crusts and white dwarf interiors), energy research (inertial confinement fusion), and technology (semiconductors). Bell-shaped (bright) and anti-bell-shaped (dark) solitons arise as a result of a balance between cubic nonlinearity and quantum dispersion. Localized peaks are bright solitons, simulating compressive pulses, while dips are dark solitons simulating rarefactive pulses. These are particularly notable in spin-polarized or electron-deficient quantum plasmas and find applications in maintaining signal quality in quantum well devices and in enabling high-accuracy electrostatic diagnostics during laser-produced plasmas. Kink and anti-kink solitons are smooth topological transitions connecting two distinct constant states. From a physical perspective, they serve as models for quantum ion-acoustic shock waves or traveling boundaries within plasmas, supported by quantum phenomena such as the Bohm potential and quantum pressure. These structures are useful for understanding sheath formation in nanoscale plasmas, sudden potential changes in quantum diodes, and interface dynamics in layered quantum devices, especially where transverse confinement (in the yz plane) aids in preserving wave stability throughout propagation. W-shaped solitons are symmetric about the middle reflect the presence of higher-order nonlinearities or mutually competing dispersion effects in NLEQZK model. Such multi-peak structures could represent bound soliton state, modulated wave packets, or quantum interference patterns, particularly in anisotropic or strongly interacting quantum systems. Such solitons are of special interest in quantum plasma crystals and ultra-cold Fermi gas research, where similar multi-peak structures were also observed both theoretically and experimentally. Periodic wave solutions, similar to conventional cnoidal waves, represent stable oscillatory structures that do not collapse from quantum diffraction and nonlinear dispersion. These solutions are useful in understanding modulational instability, energy transfer, and the initial stages of soliton splitting in confined or magnetized quantum plasmas.In addition to standard soliton forms (kink, bright, dark), this study uncovers new exact solutions for the NLEQZK equation that prior work has not reported. These comprise Weierstrass doubly periodic waves (Case 3), Jacobi elliptic solutions (Cases 2, 5), and complex hybrid forms. The IMETFM’s flexible auxiliary equation makes this range possible. It provides wave behavior in quantum magnetized plasmas, covering periodic vibrations to overlapping wave groups. Collectively, these analytically solved and graphically presented solutions highlight the complex nonlinear dynamics summarized under NLEQZK equation. The model proves significantly effective in describing coherent, multidimensional wave propagation in quantum plasmas and unlocks new avenues for both theoretical exploration and practical applications. Possible applications include nanoscale electronics, modulational control of nonlinear waves in quantum fluids, and simulation of large-scale electrostatic oscillations in dense astrophysical systems, greatly expanding the frontiers of quantum plasma science in fundamental as well as applied directions.

Comparison

As illustrated in Table 2, current analytical techniques used for the NLEQZK equation, including NMM, SSM, MEAEM, ESE, modified F-expansion, GKM and MKM26,27, have been able to produce numerous soliton and periodic solutions, including bright, dark, kink and singular profiles, mainly by means of simpler auxiliary equations such as Riccati or exponential terms. They generally use elementary auxiliary equations such as Riccati or exponential terms but are inherently restricted to the generation of a single periodic or localized wave form. They are typically unable to enter the more advanced doubly periodic solutions regime. By contrast, our IMETFM method, based on a generalized auxiliary ODE. This method consistently produces a wider class of solutions, including Jacobi elliptic functions (sn, cn, dn) and Weierstrass elliptic functions. Though the algebraic complexity is increased and requires symbolic calculation, the resulting solutions are mathematically deeper and provide more comprehensive description of wave dynamics in quantum plasmas, with the potential to capture coherent, lattice like ion-acoustic behaviors under strong magnetization. To our knowledge, this is the first stand-alone application of this method to the NLEQZK equation, generating not only new solutions but also setting a new standard for analytical completeness within Bifurcation

In this section, the Bifurcation analysis of Eq. (6) is performed. Bifurcation analysis is a powerful mathematical tool used to study the changes in the qualitative structure and stability of solutions of dynamical systems, particularly nonlinear systems, as system parameters vary. It identifies critical parameter values (bifurcation points) at which the system transitions from one type of behavior to another, such as from stability to instability or from a steady state to oscillatory or chaotic dynamics. By using Galilean transformation, planar dynamical system of Eq. (6) is:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{d u}{d \xi } = N, \\ \frac{d N}{d \xi } = -L_3 u^3 - L_2 u^2 + u L_1, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1 = \frac{\omega }{\mu ^3 c + \mu d (\delta ^2 + \beta ^2)}, L_2 = \frac{a}{2 \left( d (\beta ^2 + \delta ^2) + c \mu ^2 \right) }$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_3=\frac{b}{3 \left( d (\beta ^2 + \delta ^2) + c \mu ^2 \right) }$$\end{document}. The Planar system (18) can be written in the Hamiltonian form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{d u}{d \xi } = \frac{\partial H}{\partial N}, \\ \frac{d N}{d \xi } = -\frac{\partial H}{\partial u}. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The Hamiltonian function is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(u, N) = \frac{N^2}{2}+ V(u), \text {where} \quad \frac{\partial H}{\partial u}=\frac{d V}{d u}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Matching the second equation in system (18) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\frac{\partial H}{\partial u}$$\end{document} gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\frac{d V}{d u} = -L_3 u^3 - L_2 u^2 + u L_1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}by integrating, the potential function V(u) is obtained\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V(u) = \int (L_3 u^3 + L_2 u^2 - u L_1)du, \\ V(u) = -\frac{u^2 L_1}{2} + \frac{u^3 L_2}{3} + \frac{u^4 L_3}{4}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hence, the Hamiltonian can be written explicitly as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(u, N) = \frac{N^2}{2} - \frac{u^2 L_1}{2} + \frac{u^3 L_2}{3} + \frac{u^4 L_3}{4}= h, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where h represent Hamiltonian constant or energy level, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{N^2}{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{u^4 L_3}{4}- \frac{u^2 L_1}{2} + \frac{u^3 L_2}{3}$$\end{document} represent kinetic and potential energy, respectively. Now, phase portrait of system (18) will be studied.A phase portrait is a graphical representation of the trajectories of a dynamical system in its phase space. Each point in this space represents a state of the system defined by variables such as position and velocity or field values and their derivatives. By plotting trajectories, phase portraits illustrate how the system evolves over time from various initial conditions. Phase portraits reveals important characteristics, such as equilibrium points( system remains steady) and nature of points (center, saddle, cuspidal). In bifurcation analysis, phase portraits help to visualize how these features change as system parameters are varied. Changes in the number and stability type of equilibria indicate bifurcations. By using the different sign combinations, different cases are arise, which determine equilibrium points are either center, saddle or cuspidal. System (18) has equilibrium points:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0=(0, 0), R_1=(M_1, 0),R_2=(M_2, 0),$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1=\frac{-L_2 +\sqrt{L_2^2 + 4 L_1 L_3}}{2 L_3}, M_2=\frac{-L_2 -\sqrt{L_2^2 + 4 L_1 L_3}}{2 L_3}.$$\end{document} The determinant of Jacobian matrix for system (18) is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J(u,N)= \begin{vmatrix} 0&1 \\ L_1- 2 L_2 u - L_3 u^2&0 \\ \end{vmatrix} = - L_1+2 L_2 u + 3L_3 u^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Consequently, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(u,N)<0, J(u,N)<0$$\end{document} and J(u, N)=0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_i(i=0,1,2)$$\end{document} will represent saddle, center and cuspidal, respectively. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1>0, L_2>0, L_3>0$$\end{document}, we obtained \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0)<0, J(R_1)<0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_2)>0$$\end{document}. since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0), J(R_1)$$\end{document} are negative, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_1$$\end{document} represent saddle points. Ultimately, the equilibrium points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0, 0), (M_1, 0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M_2, 0)$$\end{document} are classified as center, saddle and saddle, respectively. (see Fig. 10b)

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1>0, L_2<0, L_3>0$$\end{document}, we obtained \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0)<0, J(R_1)>0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_2)<0$$\end{document}. since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0), J(R_2)$$\end{document} are negative, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_2$$\end{document} represent saddle points. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_1)$$\end{document} is positive, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_1$$\end{document} represent center point. Ultimately, the equilibrium points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0, 0), (M_1, 0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M_2, 0)$$\end{document} are classified as saddle, center and saddle, respectively. (see Fig. 10c)

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1>0, L_2<0, L_3>0$$\end{document}, we obtained \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0)<0, J(R_1)>0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_2)<0$$\end{document}. since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0), J(R_2)$$\end{document} are negative, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_2$$\end{document} represent saddle points. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_1)$$\end{document} is positive, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_1$$\end{document} represent center point. Ultimately, the equilibrium points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0, 0), (M_1, 0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M_2, 0)$$\end{document} are classified as saddle, center and saddle, respectively. (see Fig. 10c)

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1<0, L_2>0, L_3<0$$\end{document}, we obtained \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0)>0, J(R_1)>0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_2)<0$$\end{document}. since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_2)$$\end{document} is negative, then point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_2$$\end{document} is saddle Point. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_1)$$\end{document} are positive, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0,R_1$$\end{document} represent center points. Ultimately, the equilibrium points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0, 0), (M_1, 0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M_2, 0)$$\end{document} are classified as center, center and saddle, respectively. (see Fig. 10d)

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1<0, L_2>0, L_3<0$$\end{document}, we obtained \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0)>0, J(R_1)>0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_2)<0$$\end{document}. since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_2)$$\end{document} is negative, then point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_2$$\end{document} is saddle Point. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(R_1)$$\end{document} are positive, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0,R_1$$\end{document} represent center points. Ultimately, the equilibrium points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0, 0), (M_1, 0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M_2, 0)$$\end{document} are classified as center, center and saddle, respectively. (see Fig. 10d)

Phase diagrams for different cases.

These bifurcation diagrams provide a roadmap for predicting how the system’s behavior changes as physical parameters vary, and establish the foundation for identifying parameter regimes where more complex dynamics, including chaos, may emerge.

Chaotic behaviour

Chaotic behaviour in nonlinear dynamical systems often emerges through bifurcations, which are critical transitions where system stability changes qualitatively with parameter variation. Recent studies have shown that bifurcation analysis enables identification of such transitions leading to chaos by transforming complex equations into lower-dimensional dynamical systems and examining their phase portraits and perturbations. To understand the behaviour, a perturbed term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_0 \cos ({{E}} \xi )$$\end{document} is introduced in the system (18), yields19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{d u}{d \xi } = N, \\ \frac{d N}{d \xi } = -L_3 u^3 - L_2 u^2 + u L_1 + s_0 \cos \left( E \,\xi \right) , \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_0$$\end{document} and E represent frequency and amplitude. Now we will examine how the system behave for different values of E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_0$$\end{document}. Figures 12, 13 and 14 show the chaotic behaviour for different values of E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_0$$\end{document}. Time-series plot, 3D phase and 2D phase portrait are plotted. To quantitatively support the presence of chaotic dynamics, the largest Lyapunov exponent (LLE) is computed for selected parameter sets. The LLE measures the average exponential divergence of nearby trajectories in phase space; a positive value indicates chaos. The computed LLE values are presented in Table 3, where all cases exhibit positive exponents, thereby confirming the chaotic behavior observed in the phase portraits and time-series plots. This quantitative analysis strengthens the reliability of the chaotic interpretation.Fig. 12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{2}}D$$\end{document} phase, 3D phase and time series plots illustrate the chaotic behaviour of system (18) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=0.2$$\end{document}.Fig. \begin{document}$$s_0=4$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Sensitivity analysis

To illustrate the sensitivity of the equation, the dynamical planar system is considered based on the Galilean transformation given in Eq. (19). Sensitivity analysis describes how the system responds to small variations in its initial conditions. If small changes in initial conditions lead to minor variations in system behavior, the system is considered less sensitive. However, if small perturbations result in significant deviations, the system exhibits strong sensitivity. In this section, sensitivity is analyzed by considering different sets of initial conditions. The corresponding time-series responses are presented in Figures 15, 16, 17 and 18. All sensitivity plots are presented with clearly labeled axes, where the horizontal axis represents time t and the vertical axis represents the amplitude u, ensuring clarity, consistency, and reproducibility of the results. Conclusion

The IMETFM is used to investigate the NLEQZK equation. By employing quartic auxiliary ODE, our method enhances the diversity and structural complexity of solutions obtained. It generates not only traditional soliton profiles but also doubly periodic waves and multi-wave interaction patterns, which are essential for fully describing the physical behavior of nonlinear systems in higher dimensions. Discovering these multi-wave structures (such as solitons, breather-like waves, and doubly periodic forms) in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(3+1)$$\end{document}-dimensional model holds major importance for magnetized plasma and fluid systems. There, intricate wave interactions drive energy transfer, turbulence initiation, and the emergence of coherent structures. Beyond revealing these structurally rich solutions for magnetized systems, our framework also enables deeper dynamical investigation. By bifurcation analysis, we see how the wave patterns evolve with changes in the system parameters. Further, the detection of chaotic regimes and sensitivity to initial conditions establish the presence of turbulence and erratic dynamics. These results are of direct relevance to practical applications in plasma physics, geophysical fluid dynamics, and nonlinear optics.The NLEQZK equation is crucial for capturing transverse magnetic-field effects and multidirectional wave propagation in magnetized quantum fluid systems. Nonlinear self-interaction, quantum diffraction, and magnetic dispersion govern the stability, localization, and evolution of wave structures. This multidimensional approach offers realistic insights into wave dynamics in dense astrophysical plasmas, fusion devices, space weather, and other environments where transverse effects matter. While the computational expense of this procedure is greater than for more straightforward analytical methods, the increased physical understanding and solution diversity justify this expenditure. We propose that contemporary analytical techniques should extend beyond the classical goal of merely discovering solutions. Rather, they should be used to reveal the inner dynamical aspects of nonlinear models. In the future, this approach can be applied to other relativistic hydrodynamics, quantum plasmas, and Bose-Einstein condensates high-dimensional nonlinear systems. This method offers a more effective and expanded set of tools to model, analyze, and even control intricate wave behavior in physical and engineering systems.
